### Page 1

3-month preview: Landmark’s Sep revenues were up 190% YoY/ 4% MoM to 
NT$540m, supported by strong demand on 800G / 1.6T SiPh optical modules and 
better InP substrate supply. Looking ahead, we expect company’s Oct/ Nov/ Dec 
revenues to see sequential MoM growth at 5%/ 5%/ 8%, resulting in 4Q revenues 
growth at 16% QoQ to NT$1.8bn, with company’s continuous capacity expansion.

### Page 2

Earnings revision: We factor in Landmark 3Q26 result and revise up earnings by 9%/ 
3%/ 2%/ 1% in 2026-29E mainly on higher revenues of SiPh CW lasers, supported by 
strong client demand and capacity expansion. We revise up GM by 1ppts in 2026E on 
mix upgrade, and revise down Opex ratio in 2026-29E to reflect improving operational 
efficiency amid the larger scale.

Valuation: We employ a discounted P/E methodology (unchanged) and apply a 61.5x 
(unchanged, report link) target P/E multiple on our 2029E EPS, discounted back to 
2027E at a COE of 10.5% (beta 1.7x, risk free rate 1.6%, and market risk premium at 
5.1% - unchanged). Our target P/E multiple of 61.5x is derived from the PEG&M ratio of 
peers (unchanged) in the photonics supply chain. Consequently, our 12-month target 
price is now NT$4,490 (vs. previously at NT$4,459). Our new TP implied 2028E P/E of 
75x is between company’s avg. P/E at 62x and avg. +1 P/E at 91x. Maintain Buy.

### Page 3

Price Target Risks and Methodology - LandMark 
Price Target Risks and Methodology - LandMark Optoelectronics Corp.  
Valuation: We employ a discounted P/E methodology, applying a 61.5x target P/E 
multiple on our 2029E EPS, then discount it back to 2027E at a COE of 10.5% (beta 1.7x, 
risk free rate 1.6% and market risk premium at 5.1%), to derive our 12-month target 
price of NT$4,490. Our target P/E multiple is based on the same PEG&M ratio as peers in 
the photonics supply chain. We are Buy-rated on LandMark. 
Key risk: Slower-than-expected SiPh penetration; stronger-than-expected competition; 
Integrated Device Manufacturers (IDMs) offering Epitaxial (EPI) wafers in-house.

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.
Not Rated (NR). The investment rating, target price and earnings estimates (where relevant) are removed pursuant to Goldman Sachs policy when
Goldman Sachs is acting in an advisory capacity in a merger or in a strategic transaction involving this company, when there are legal, regulatory or
policy constraints due to Goldman Sachs’ involvement in a transaction, and in certain other circumstances. Early-Stage Biotech (ES). An investment

rating and a target price are not assigned pursuant to Goldman Sachs policy when this company has neither a drug, treatment or medical device that

주가 추이

3Q 큰 폭의 판매 감소, 매출/영업이익 시장 기대치 하회 예상 
3Q26 동사 매출액과 영 업 이 익 은 각각 44.6조원(-4.5% 이하 Y oY)과 2.4조원
(-5.4%) 기록하며 시장 기대치 하회 전망. 3Q 매출액은 글로벌 판매 부진 지속
과 환율 하락, 그리고 친환경차 판매 성장 둔화 등의 영향에 기인. 특히, 3Q 판
매량은 918.7만대(-11.5% 이하 Y oY)로 전년 동기 대비 큰 폭 감소하며 매출
액에 부정적 영향. 이는 7월과 8월 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생
산 물량 차질이 발 생 한 부분에 기인. 파업은 기존 물량/믹스 개선에 기 여 했 던 
글로벌 HEV 판매도 둔화(+3.4%)시키면서 매출에 부정적 영향을 심화. 
3Q 수 익 성 은 4~5월 상승했던 원자재 투입에 따른 원가 상승 영향과 친환경차 
판매 감소에 따른 부정적 믹스 영향 불 가 피. 이에 시장 기대치인 3.0조원을 크
게 하회할 전망. 다만, 기말 환율이 1,356원/$ 수준으로 낮아지면서 판매보증비 
외화환산손익 영향은 다소 개선(0.4%p) 예상되며, 관세 영향 또한 전년 동기 
대비 약 1조원(2.1%p) 감소하면서 수익성 악화 영향을 일부 만 회 할 전망.  
’27년 지연된 신차효과 가시화로 반등 여력 충분 
4Q부터는 3Q 파업 영향 등으로 지연된 신차 확대 본격화되고, 현지 수요 높은 
차종 중심의 물량 확대를 통해 매출 회복 및 이익 성장 가시화가 기대. 아 반 떼
와 투싼 FMC 확대로 국내 및 해외 판매 둔화가 점진적 만회되고, 지난 9월부
터 터키에서 아이오닉3 생산이 본격화되면서 유럽에서의 시장 부진 회복이 기
대. 또한, GV80과 G80 HEV 모델 출시로 내년부터 국내/북미 중심 판매 확대
되며 올해 부진했던 물량/믹스 개선에 기여할 것으로 기대. ’27년 HMGMA에
서 HEV 신차 생산 투입 예정되어 있어 상 대 적 으 로 높은 수요가 지속되는 북미
에서의 HEV 현지 생산 확대로 가동률 증 가 와 관세 부담 완화도 수익성 개선에 
기여할 것으로 기대. 
부정적 환율 고려 필요하나 ’27년 강한 증익 기대  
’27년 동사 영 업 이 익 추 정 치 는 12.4조원(+22.3%)으로 ’26년 감 익 에 서 ’27년 
증익 전환 예상. 최근 환율 추세 고려 시 내년 상 반 기 까 지 부정적인 환율 영향 
확대 가 능 성 존재하나, 기존 노후 차 종 에 서 현지 수요 대응 가능한 신차 중심 
생산/판매 확대로 물량/믹스 개선되며 부 정 적 환율 영향 상쇄 기대.  
12M EPS 추정치 하향(-11.8%)과 상반기 적 용 한 코 스 피 프리미엄 제거(-
10%)에 따라 목 표 주 가 를 670,000원으로 하향. 그러나, 연 내 RMAC 운영 
성 과 와 RA 설립, 12 월 모셔널 자 율 주 행 상용화와 웨 이 모向 로 보 택 시 
양산/공급 등 미래 사 업 에 서 의 가 시 성 도 여전히 기대 가능한 부분.

[ Compliance Notice ] (공표일: 2026 년 10월 8 일) 
이 자료는 조사분석 담당자가 객관적 사실에 근거해 작성하였으며, 타인의 부당한 압력이나 간섭없이 본인의 견을 정확하게 
반영했습니다. 본인은 이 자료에서 다룬 종목과 관련해 공표일 현재 관련 법규상 알려야 할 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 본인은 이 
자료를 기관투자자 또는 제 3 자에게 사전에 제공한 사실이 없습니다. (김성래) 
저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
이 자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위해 당사 고객에 한하여 배포되는 자료로서 저작권이 당사에 있으며 불법 복제 및 배포를 금
합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 8

현대차 (005380) [한화리서치]

8 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

[ 당사 조사분석자료의 투자등급 부여 비중 ] (기준일: 2026 년 9 월 30 일)

Xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn hiện vẫn đang được bảo lưu và VNINDEX đư ợc kỳ vọng 
sẽ có nhịp hồi phục trở lại khi về đến vùng hỗ trợ kế tiếp tại quanh 1710

Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -49.02 điểm tương ứng -2.66%, với thanh khoản cải 
thiện. Dệt may, Y tế và Bất động sản là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt nhất trong 
tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -4,944 tỷ đồng, 
tập trung ở các mã VPB, TCB và VHM; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở mức 1995 
tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại bán ròng -5,280 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần qua và đang 
duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế 3,630 hợp đồng. Tự doanh mua ròng 208 hợp đồng nhưng đang 
duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -6,428 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam tiếp tục điều chỉnh tuần thứ hai liên tiếp sau nâng hạng, khi VN-Index lùi 
về đóng cửa 1.737 điểm — giảm khoảng 2% so với tuần trước và đang kiểm định lại vùng 
đáy cũ của tháng 8, quanh 1.720. Áp lực bán duy trì khi chỉ số giảm nhiều phiên liên tiếp, 
cho thấy hiệu ứng "mua kỳ vọng, bán thực tế" sau nâng hạng vẫn chưa kết thúc, trong bối 
cảnh dòng tiền thận trọng và thanh khoản ở mức thấp. Khối ngoại tiếp tục là lực cản 
chính khi bán ròng mạnh cả 5 phiên trong tuần, riêng phiên 2/10 bán ròng tới 3.361 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở PNJ và HDB. Áp lực bên ngoài vẫn hiện diện khi Fed duy trì lập trường 
diều hâu và giá dầu ở mức cao; trong một, hai tuần tới, mùa báo cáo KQKD sẽ bắt đầu có 
tác động, kỳ vọng theo chiều hướng tích cực, qua đó giúp nâng đỡ thị trường chung.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX hình thành cây nến giảm điểm tiêu cực trên khung tuần, xuyên thủng vùng hỗ trợ 
gần quanh 1750, cho thấy áp lực bán đã có phần quyết liệt hơn sau 2 tuần giằng co trước 
đó. Trên khung ngày, sau các phiên lình xình vào đầu tuần, chỉ số cũng dần chuyển sang 
diễn biến giảm điểm rõ nét hơn về cuối tuần, đồng thời cắt sâu xuống dưới SMA50, thể 
hiện áp lực phân phối đang có phần lấn át mặc dù chưa xảy ra hiện tượng bán tháo khi 
khối lượng khớp lệnh được giữ ở mức thấp. Diễn biến tính từ vùng đỉnh 1870 vào đầu tháng 
9 đang không mấy khả quan, tuy nhiên xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn, tính từ đáy 1640 vào cuối 
tháng 7, hiện vẫn đang được bảo lưu và VNINDEX được kỳ vọng sẽ có nhịp hồi phục trở lại 
khi về đến vùng hỗ trợ kế tiếp tại quanh 1710. 
Kháng cự xa: 1820 (+-15)

Hỗ trợ gần: 1710 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1670 (+-15)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

## Document 312: Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower_.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower?
Date:    1 hour ago
Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower?

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Could Midterms Give Wall Street
A Cold Shower?
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 2 Nov 2026
View in browser

I. When Gridlock Isn't Good for Stocks
The US midterm elections will be over tomorrow night. According to
betting markets, Democrats have roughly a 95% probability of winning the
House and a 76% probability of taking the Senate (charts).
We suspect that stock investors have yet to fully discount the possibility of
a Democratic sweep of Congress. Some may remain skeptical that polls
confirming strong support for Democrats are accurate. They might reckon

### Page 2

that Democrats are more likely than Republicans to talk to pollsters over
the phone.
In any event, Wall Street traditionally welcomes political gridlock because
it reduces the likelihood that Congress will pass extremist legislation. But
this time could be different for two reasons.
First, deepening partisanship could turn gridlock into damaging political
paralysis at a time when the Bond Vigilantes are already uneasy about
Washington's unsustainable fiscal trajectory. A Democratic sweep could

complicate negotiations over government spending and the debt ceiling.
Prolonged shutdowns and fiscal standoffs could push Treasury yields
higher, weighing on equity valuations.

### Page 3

Second, gridlock's traditional ability to constrain policymaking may not
hold this time. President Donald Trump has relied heavily on executive
authority, with some of his most market-disruptive policies, including tariffs
and military action against Iran, pursued largely without congressional
approval. A Democratic-controlled Congress may therefore do little to
restrain him. Moreover, with the midterms behind him, Trump may feel
even less constrained politically and more willing to pursue controversial

policies through executive action. Hence, a blue wave could deliver a cold
shower for stocks, though we would view a significant selloff as a buying
opportunity given the remarkable strength of the economy and corporate
earnings.
One encouraging development ahead of the elections was today's reports
that the US and Iran are working on a peaceful diplomatic solution to their
war. Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian said he has granted his
government greater authority to pursue peace talks, while President

Trump and Vice President Vance also signaled progress. Hopes for a deal
sent the price of Brent crude oil down 7.6% to $104.77 per barrel (chart).
However, we have been here before with apparent diplomatic
breakthroughs. We still see a high risk of military re-escalation. The bid-
ask spread remains wide, with Tehran demanding sanctions relief, the
release of frozen assets, and an end to the US naval blockade of Iranian
ports, while Washington insists on nuclear concessions. Meanwhile, Iran's

leverage over the Strait of Hormuz has been weakening. According to
shipping data, for roughly half of September, Gulf oil exporters shipped
more crude than they were shipping before the war.

### Page 4

II. US Economy Continues To Roar
The US economy remains strong despite the volatility of the domestic
political and geopolitical scenes. Consider the following:
(1) Construction spending. Construction spending fell 1.9% m/m in
September to $2.19 trillion (saar), reversing most of August's upwardly
revised 2.0% increase. Nevertheless, spending remains just below last
year's record high (chart).
September's decline was driven by a 5.5% m/m drop in private residential

construction, attributable to a 13.0% plunge in home improvements
(chart). Elevated materials costs and higher mortgage rates continue to
weigh on the housing sector. Meanwhile, private nonresidential
construction rose 1.1% m/m, supported by continued investment in data
centers and power infrastructure.

### Page 5

The AI investment boom remains a powerful tailwind. Data center
construction spending surged 8.6% m/m and 90% y/y in September,
reaching a record $96.5 billion annual rate (chart).
(2) Manufacturing. The ISM M-PMI rose from 54.5 in September to 55.5
in October, marking its tenth consecutive month of expansion, the longest
streak since 2022. Production jumped to the highest reading since
November 2021, while new orders edged up to 55.5. Employment also

remained in expansionary territory (chart).

### Page 6

Inflationary pressures remain troublesome. The prices-paid index eased
from 77.9 to 75.2 in October but remained above 70 for the ninth
consecutive month. Regional Fed surveys also point to persistent price
pressures. Higher energy costs, tariffs, and elevated metals prices
continue to boost input costs (chart).
(3) Bank lending. The Fed's latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey
(SLOOS) painted a mixed picture of credit demand. Banks reported
weaker demand for commercial and industrial (C&I) loans, mortgages,

and auto loans in Q4, while credit-card demand strengthened (chart).

### Page 7

Lending standards remained broadly unchanged for C&I loans but
tightened somewhat for commercial real estate loans (chart).
Encouragingly, actual bank lending tells a happier story. C&I loans surged
11.3% y/y through October 21, while total loans and leases increased
7.9% y/y (chart).

### Page 8

Credit growth is broad across bank sizes, with lending up 8.1% y/y at
large banks and 5.4% at small banks (chart).
Meanwhile, banks' allowances for loan and lease losses have remained
relatively stable at around $202 billion, despite the acceleration in lending
(chart).

### Page 9

clause.

U.S. Week Ahead
Existing Home Sales • Thursday
Housing market conditions remain highly unfavorable. Mortgage
rates are the primary headwind, and they have climbed even higher
alongside the rise in longer-term yields. At the same time, low
supply is preventing a broad correction in home prices, leaving
prospective buyers squeezed on two fronts.
We expect that existing home sales slid 3.4% in August to an
annualized rate of 3.92 million. Behind this forecast is ongoing

weakness in mortgage applications and a recent bounce in
mortgage rates, which rose from 6.43% at the beginning of July to
6.66% by month-end.
We do not expect affordability conditions to meaningfully improve
in the near term. Supply is likely to remain constrained while high
mortgage rates discourage mobility. And mortgage rates are apt
to remain elevated amid firm economic growth expectations and
heavy government and corporate debt issuance.

CPI • Friday
Headline inflation looks to have picked up last month. We estimate
the CPI rose 0.40% in August, with gasoline prices increasing a little
over 4% as renewed tensions in the Middle East pushed oil higher.
Grocery prices also likely rebounded after modestly declining in July.
Outside of food and energy, price pressures appear little changed.
We expect the core CPI to rise 0.23%, essentially matching July's

pace. Core goods inflation should remain firm, led by another solid
increase in used vehicle prices as consumer prices continue to
catch up to the trend indicated at wholesale auctions. Meantime,
household furnishings and other tariff-sensitive categories appear
to be stabilizing after declining earlier this year.
Core services inflation should be little changed with another 0.2%
monthly increase. But underneath the surface, we expect a modest
pickup in travel (led by lodging away from home) to offset slightly

cooler medical care and housing cost growth.
All told, we expect the report to show that while headline inflation
remains influenced by the ebbs and flows of the conflict in the
Middle East, core inflation remains contained. Should our forecast
prove correct, core CPI will ease to 2.4% year-over-year, in line with
its 6-month annualized rate but above the more recent 3-month
annualized pace of 1.7%.

### Page 4

Weekly Economics
G10 Week Ahead
European Central Bank Monetary Policy Meeting •
Thursday
We expect the European Central Bank (ECB) to raise the Deposit
Rate by 25 bps to 2.50% next week. August headline inflation
remained elevated at 3.3% year-over-year, though softer core
and services inflation suggest underlying pressures continue to
moderate. Still, the more hawkish tone of the July minutes and
renewed rise in energy prices should keep policymakers cautious.

The recent escalation in the Middle East has pushed fuel prices
at the pump close to their 2022 peaks, while natural gas storage
remains below historical levels, leaving the Eurozone vulnerable
to further energy price increases. The ECB is unlikely to respond
aggressively if the shock remains confined to energy given the
downside risks to growth. However, a broader rise in food inflation
linked to El Niño would be more concerning, as highly visible prices

could lift inflation expectations and generate second-round effects.
For now, resilient Q3 activity, elevated headline inflation and
contained wage growth support one final hike. The ECB will likely
retain and emphasize its scenario-based outlook in the updated
staff projections given continued uncertainty around the conflict
and energy prices. The policy path beyond September will depend
on whether energy and food pressures persist and begin to spread,

which could leave room for further tightening.

Mexico CPI • Wednesday
We expect Mexico’s August inflation rate to edge higher, with
headline CPI rising to 3.34% year-over-year (0.27% month-over-
month) from 3.26% year-over-year in early August. Part of the
pickup likely reflects less favorable base effects and a smaller
disinflationary contribution from food prices. While agricultural
prices continued to help restrain inflation in mid-August, the drag

was notably less pronounced than in July. Core inflation should
remain elevated at 3.98% year-over-year (0.25% month-over-
month).
While inflation remains within Banxico’s midpoint 3% target,
persistent core and services inflation suggest convergence toward
the midpoint of the target could prove gradual. For now, we
continue to expect Banxico to keep rates on hold, with the policy
rate remaining at 6.50% through the rest of 2026 and into early
2027 as policymakers await clearer evidence that underlying

inflation pressures are easing sustainably. While rate cuts remain
a possibility in 2027, the current inflation backdrop argues for
patience in the near term.

Brazil CPI • Friday
We expect Brazil’s August inflation rate to slow to 4.20% year-over-
year, helped by lower electricity, food and fuel prices. The main
driver should be a temporary reduction in residential electricity bills
stemming from a one-off credit, while recent data already point to
a notable easing in price pressures. Mid-August CPI came in weaker
than expected, with inflation falling to 4.24% year-over-year amid
declines in electricity, food, fuel and airfare prices. Beyond these

temporary factors, restrictive monetary policy continues to weigh
on economic activity, as evidenced by the recent slowdown in Q2
GDP growth. However, underlying inflation risks remain. A still-
tight labor market and government measures aimed at supporting
consumption could keep services inflation sticky, making progress
toward the 3.0% inflation target more gradual.
Overall, the inflation outlook remains consistent with additional
policy easing. We expect COPOM to deliver a 25 bps rate cut in

September, bringing the Selic Rate to 13.75%. Beyond September,
policymakers are likely to proceed cautiously as they assess
incoming inflation data and the impact of the election cycle on
inflation expectations. Our base case remains for a pause after
September, with additional easing more likely in 2027.

## Document 365: LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI  investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

LatAm’s silicon dividend: 
tailwinds from the AI  
investment boom in the U.S.

1 
Authors 
Fancisco Campos 
Chief LatAm Economist

Beatriz Garcia Nunes 
LatAm Economist

October 28, 2026 
Key messages: 
- U.S. investment has broken away from Europe and China since 2023, 
powered by AI-related equipment, software and research spending. 
- The AI CapEx boom demands commodities and merchandise that LatAm 
can supply.  
- Latin America’s export gains are real but concentrated. Copper, computer 
and electronics products provide the clearest links to the U.S. AI buildout. 
- Chile is the region’s most direct copper beneficiary. Mexico captures the 
windfall stemming from increases hardware demand and trade-diversion. In 
these cases, the AI-related tailwinds have undergirded appreciable 
improvements in external accounts. 
- The cases for nickel and zinc illustrate that the dividend is not widespread. 
U.S. imports of the former have mostly returned toward pre-pandemic levels, 
while for the latter bilateral gains partly reflect supplier substitution. 
- While expanding AI use, supply shortages and incentives can extend the 
CapEx boom, its financial viability poses downside risks. 
Another ‘American exceptionalism’ story  
Investment has become one of the sharpest dividing lines among the world’s 
core economic blocs. Since 2024, U.S. gross fixed capital formation has firmed 
up appreciably. Meanwhile, investment in Europe has broadly stalled while in 
China’s it has retreated, the drivers of which are beyond the scope of this report 
(Figure 1).  
The U.S. divergence has largely been an AI story: a burst of spending on 
computing power, digital infrastructure and intellectual property has offset 
weakness in conventional construction and housing. Indeed, total U.S. fixed 
investment is nearly 12% above its 2023 level. Within it, investment in 
information-processing equipment has risen by roughly 57%, software outlays by 
about 35%, and research and development by around 15%. By contrast, non-
residential structures and residential investment sit below their 2023 levels 
(Figure 2).

### Page 2

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: The global investment divergence

Figure 2: AI-related categories lead U.S. investment

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Eurostat, National Bureau of Statistics of 
China, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Latin America’s commodity channel 
AI is not merely a semiconductor story. Chips require specialized minerals; data 
centers require electricity and cooling; electricity requires grids, transformers and 
storage. That chain pulls in copper, aluminum, silver, tin, lithium and selected 
semiconductor inputs, along with natural gas and uranium. Latin America has 
meaningful exposure to several of these inputs, but copper has emerged as the 
commodity with the cleanest and most economically significant regional link. 
Copper is used throughout the AI infrastructure stack, including power cables, 
substations, transformers, server boards, heat sinks and cooling systems. Rising 
U.S. copper demand has lifted imports from Chile and Peru, particularly the 
former (Figure 3). T he effect matters beyond merchandise export volumes. 
Favorable terms-of-trade have helped Chile generate a current -account surplus 
for the first time in roughly fifteen years (0.2% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -
quarter rolling basis ) and have allowed Peru to log the largest surplus in about 
forty-five years (4.5% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4-quarter rolling basis).

28 October 2026

### Page 3

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Figure 3: Copper epitomized the commodity link

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Not every metal is copper 
The broader mining story demands more caution, though. U.S. nickel imports have 
recovered toward pre-Covid levels, but the upswing has not displayed the same 
persistence as copper. Mexico has captured a disproportionate share of the 
bilateral improvement, yet that gain looks more like normalization than a 
sustained AI -driven break (Figure 4). Zinc tells a similar story. U.S. real imports 
stand shy of their pre-pandemic levels, and stronger purchases from Mexico and 
Peru partly reflect a reallocation away from Brazil rather than a decisive increase 
in U.S. demand (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Nickel: recovery, not a boom

Figure 5: Zinc: a substitution story

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

28 October 2026

### Page 4

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

Mexico’s hardware advantage 
The larger non-commodity prize is in computers and electronics. U.S. imports of 
these products have sky -rocketed, while the supplier mix has shifted away from 
China. Mexico and various Asian economies have gained share on the back of this 
double-whammy impulse (Figure 6). 
This helps explain why Mexico’s non-automotive manufacturing exports, notably 
electronics, have outpaced automotive shipments to the U.S. and pressures from 
higher oil prices. The resulting non -oil trade surplus has offset pressure from the 
oil balance and helped Mexico generate a balanced current account (0% of GDP 
as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -quarter rolling basis). The external boost is meaningful, 
although it sho uld not necessarily be interpreted as bona fide  evidence of 
nearshoring, in our view.

Figure 6: U.S. computer & electronics imports going into overdrive

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research.

How much runway remains? 
Quantifying or mapping out a timeframe for the AI investment outlook is beyond 
the scope of this piece or our expertise. Nevertheless, we’d venture to argue that 
the capex cycle appears more likely to mature gradually than to stop abruptly . 
One reason is that the investment case is shifting from frontier -model training 
toward enterprise inference . Capacity that once served development can 
increasingly run commercial workloads for companies. That creates a path to 
revenue even if artificial general intelligence (AGI) crystallizes later than 
expected. It also spreads demand to a much larger corporate customer base.  
A second reason is scarcity. Graphics processing units (GPUs) remain tight, while 
power availability, grid connections, cooling equipment, construction capacity 
and data -center complexity limit how quickly supply can respond. Bottlenecks 
may therefore exte nd the cycle. The constraint is especially relevant for copper 
28 October 2026

### Page 5

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

and power infrastructure, where projects have long lead times and permitting 
hurdles. 
A third reason is strategic. AI development resembles a prisoner’s dilemma at 
both the corporate and national levels. Each participant may worry about costs, 
safety and control, yet slowing unilaterally risks surrendering technological, 
commercial or natio nal security advantages. The same tension is visible in calls 
for guardrails alongside continued spending. Competition can therefore sustain 
capex even when short-term returns are uncertain. 
We acknowledge the risks, nonetheless. Higher borrowing costs raise the hurdle 
rate for an infrastructure buildout financed partly through debt. More important, 
revenues must eventually justify the installed capital base. The bearish case is not 
that AI la cks value; it is that infrastructure spending may run ahead of 
monetization, compromising some companies’ repayment capacity. Public 
opposition to the development of AI infrastructure and/or adoption of AI 
technologies could also slow down the technology’s roll-out. 
The regional balance  
Summing up, the AI investment boom in the U .S. is delivering a dividend to parts 
of Latin America, not the region as a whole. Chile and Peru benefit through copper 
prices and export volumes. Mexico benefits through electronics manufacturing 
and the replacement of Chinese supply. For Brazil and Colombia, the AI dividend 
is rather muted. Where materialized, furthermore, the dividend supports trade 
and current accounts more clearly rather than improving the domestic 
investment. 
These dynamics should persist while U.S. AI investment remains supply -
constrained and strategically protected. If widespread adoption advances and 
the strategic race intensifies, the impulse can last. If financing costs rise further 
or monetization disappo ints, the cycle may narrow. Either way, copper and 
Mexican electronics remain the region’s most visible gauges of the U.S. AI 
investment appetite.

28 October 2026

### Page 6

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
LatAm’s silicon dividend: tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. – October 28, 2026

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the
likely pace of economic activity relative to trend three to
nine months into the future, held at –0.03% in September,
unchanged from August.

The Leading Index continues to show moderately improved
momentum compared to the middle of the year, with the
latest reads pointing to growth holding at or slightly below
trend heading into late 2026/early 2027. Growth appears
to be stabilising at a slow pace rather than stalling. That
said, both the latest fuel prices and the RBA’s September
interest rate increase are still working their way through to
the economy with impacts likely to take some of the gloss off

growth near term. Overall, Westpac expects annual growth
to come in at 1.5% for calendar 2026, a below-trend result.
The Leading Index growth rate has firmed slightly over the
last six months, rising from –0.16% back in March to –0.03%
in September. The component detail shows mixed moves over
the period. Four components have contributed more positively
with reduced drags from the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Unemployment Expectations Index (adding +0.23ppts to

the Index growth rate); the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Consumer Expectations Index (adding another +0.15ppts);
and the S&P/ASX200 (+0.15ppts) combining with a modest
lift in growth in US industrial production (adding +0.12ppts).
These positives have been partially offset by modest drags
from four previously stable components: dwelling approvals
(–0.34ppts); monthly hours worked (–0.08ppts); commodity
prices, measured in AUD terms (–0.07ppts) and the yield

spread (–0.05ppts).
Overall, the component mix speaks to a lack of direction.
Indeed, despite some large shocks over the last year, the
Leading Index growth rate has been relatively contained since
early 2025, holding in a narrow –0.3% to +0.5% range. This
compares to history which, outside of the COVID and GFC
shocks, has seen growth rates move between a much wider
–1.9% to +1.6% range. Of course, the apparent stability,
which is evident across all components of the index, may not

last but for now it suggests the economy has been stuck in a
bit of a holding pattern over the last two years.
Leading Index still tracking a touch below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines
variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken

individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

## Document 375: NZ labour market preview, Q3 2026 (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ labour market preview, Q3 2026 (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Turning point?
• We expect the unemployment rate to edge
down to 5.5% for the September quarter.
• Job numbers have been gaining momentum
in recent months, and now look to be enough
to outstrip population growth. However, the
participation rate will be the deciding factor.
• Despite cost-of-living pressures on workers,
the existing slack in the labour market is likely
to keep wage growth subdued.
• Our forecasts are a little more positive than

what the RBNZ assumed in their September
policy statement. Assuming they deliver on
an OCR hike this afternoon, the labour market
surveys could have an important bearing on
whether they follow up with another hike in
December.

The New Zealand economy has returned to growth over
the last year, despite a number of headwinds. That’s been
accompanied by some modest growth in jobs, though
to date it hasn’t been enough to prevent a further rise

in the unemployment rate. However, we think that next
Wednesday’s labour market reports could show the jobs
market reaching a turning point.
We expect the unemployment rate to ease from 5.6%
to 5.5% in the September quarter, which would mark
the first fall in almost five years. That’s not a firmly held
view, given the usual margin of uncertainty around this
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Preview of Q3 labour market surveys:
4 November, 10:45am.
28 Oct 2026 | Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
measure. But we’re now seeing consistent evidence
that jobs growth is outstripping the modest pace of
population growth. We also note that the previous result
was 5.56% in unrounded terms, so it wouldn’t take much
to tip the rounded rate down to 5.5% this time.
On the whole, our forecasts are a little stronger than
what the RBNZ assumed in its September Monetary
Policy Statement. While our unemployment rate forecast

is identical, we’re more positive on both employment
and labour force participation. Meanwhile, our pick of a
0.5% rise in the Labour Cost Index is slightly ahead of the
RBNZ’s estimate of 0.4%.
Assuming that the RBNZ delivers the OCR hike that we
expect this afternoon, we think they will be noncommittal
about the chances of another hike at the 9 December
Monetary Policy Statement. In that respect, the labour
market surveys could be important in setting market

expectations for the December review, as there aren’t
that many major data releases between now and then.
Forecast details.
We expect the Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS)
to show a 0.5% rise in employment for the September
quarter. That’s broadly in line with the Monthly
Employment Indicator (MEI), with the September release
this morning showing a third straight month of growth
in filled jobs. We note that employment growth in the
HLFS has actually outstripped the MEI for the last nine

quarters, but it’s not clear why this has been the case and
we’re wary of extrapolating it too far into the next quarter.

Employment growth of around 0.5% would be more than
enough to absorb the modest 0.3% rise in the working-
age population, and on its own would suggest a fall in

the unemployment rate. But it also comes down to the
rate of labour force participation, which we don’t really
have much guidance on. The participation rate has been
choppy over recent quarters, and in the June quarter
we saw an unusually large 0.3pt rise to 70.7%; we’re
assuming some payback this time with a more modest
0.1pt rise to 70.8%.
We’ll also be keeping an eye on the broader indicators of
slack in the labour market, although we don’t formally use

these in our forecasts. The June quarter saw a sharp rise
in the underutilisation rate from 12.9% to 13.8%. However,
we think there’s some residual seasonality in this series,
relating to one component in particular: people who are
available for work but not actively looking. In that case,
we could see a substantial pullback in the underutilisation
measure this time.
Previously we’ve characterised the labour market as a
“low-fire, low-hire” environment, with much of the rise in

unemployment reflecting the difficulty of breaking into
the workforce, rather than job losses. There are some
signs of a thaw emerging, with job vacancies picking up
from their lows over the last year and business surveys
pointing to a lift in hiring intentions. But we still expect
that it will be a long road to recovery – our current
forecasts see the unemployment rate at 5% by the end of
next year.
While cost-of-living pressures are again rearing their

head as a major concern for workers, the existing degree
of slack in the labour market will continue to make it
difficult for them to push for bigger pay increases. We
expect a 0.5% rise in the Labour Cost Index (LCI) for
the September quarter, which would nudge the annual
growth rate up slightly to 2.1%. That said, we don’t have a
lot to go on for this forecast. The MEI includes a measure
of median weekly earnings, which has shown no sign of
acceleration so far – but this is only a very loose indicator

of wage pressures, as it will also reflect any changes in
the composition of jobs and pay rates.

## Document 389: Q3 Growth in advanced economies_ solid despite shocks (2026-10-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 Growth in advanced economies_ solid despite shocks (2026-10-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 23 October 2026

EcoFlash

Q3 Growth in advanced economies: solid despite shocks

Growth looks to have held up in Q3 despite successive shocks (see our analysis), as it did in 2025 and H1 2026. A longer 
Middle East conflict has fed into more persistent inflation; drought has worsened in many countries, and interest rates 
have risen further – at the short end, with both the ECB and the Fed raising policy rates in September, and even more at 
the long end. However, our nowcasts show that growth is bending without breaking in most advanced economies. GDP 
figures (due on 29 October in the US and 30 October for the euro area and its member states) are expected to confirm 
that. In the Euro area, momentum looks stable (excluding Ireland, whose GDP is volatile). In Germany, growth has been 
uninterrupted since Q4 2025, while France is likely to have returned to growth after stagnating in H1. In the US, growth 
is likely to have gathered pace, according to the GDP Now, still driven by AI, but increasingly by other sectors as well.

Euro area: resilience beneath the surface. Our nowcast points to growth slowing to 0.3% in q/q, though the pace is stable 
excluding Ireland (Irish GDP is volatile and had markedly boosted the Q2 figure). Excluding Ireland, growth should hold at its H2 
average, a sign of the bloc’s underlying resilience. Also, momentum has broadened to a growing number of sectors ( see our 
analysis). While industrial production stalled over the summer, services remained buoyant. The services PMI rebounded sharply 
in Q3 and sits firmly in expansion territory (53.0 in September). Improving job-related survey data (PMI and European Commission 
surveys) and a stable unemployment rate (6.4% in August) hint at more hiring in Q3 – a support for household consumption that 
partly offsets purchasing-power losses from persistent inflation. Corporate investment is underpinned by buoyant new investment 
lending (8.7% y/y In August, 12-month cumulative). 
France: growing, nevertheless. The succession of shocks does not appear to have prevented France from returning to 
growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q, according to our nowcast. One-off factors accounted for the stall in activity in H1. In Q3, the drought 
worsened and industrial production remained subdued. However, market services production rebounded in July and August, 
notably in business services, restaurants, digital and real estate. The latter points to residential investment recovering after a weak 
H1. Corporate investment likely followed, as reflected in buoyant credit flows and AI-related investment. Household consumption 
is likely to have improved slightly on higher electric vehicle sales.  
Euro area  France

Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas  Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas

Germany: growth still solid. The H1 rebound in activity is moderating but continues. Our nowcast points to a fourth straight 
quarter of growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q (after 0.3% in Q4 2025, 0.4% in Q1 2026, and 0.3% in Q2). Manufacturing activity eased 
slightly, according to the September PMI survey (53.9, down 0.4 m/m). Government spending will remain growth-supportive, with 
defence and infrastructure outlays gaining traction. Infrastructure spending continues to underpin activity in public works, which 
stands at its highest since November 2021 (2.5% 3m/3m in August). Exports (4.4% y/y between January and August) remain the 
main growth engine, helped by a pivot towards Europe (7.2%). 
United States: an expansion undeterred by inflation. Growth is likely to have gathered pace in Q3. The Atlanta Fed’s GDP 
Now points to a 3.5% annualized growth rate (AR), close to our own 3.7% forecast (0.9% non -AR) and up from 2.2% in 
Q2. Domestic demand remains the engine. Consumption is weathering the pickup in inflation, while the AI-driven capex cycle 
remains robust – as reflected in retail sales and nondefence capital-goods orders (excl. aircraft). As a result, growth in final sales

### Page 2

to private domestic purchasers 1 should print above 4% AR again. By contrast, external trade should remain a drag on growth, 
reflecting AI-related import needs. Survey data point to mome ntum that is both solid and broad -based. The ISM indices have 
remained firmly in growth territory (54.9 quarterly average for manufacturing and services alike). At the same time, they highlight 
persistent price and supply-chain pressures.

Germany   United States

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

Wearing thin

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

### Page 5

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... and RBA policy rates outlook
In Australia, the RBA hiked the cash rate again at its late
September meeting, and a further hike at the November
meeting looks likely. This would take the cash rate to 4.85%, a
new cycle high.
While the forecasts the RBA published in August did not

support further rate hikes, in September these forecasts were
characterised as resting on several things going right and
certain risks not emerging. Some of these risks, including a re-
escalation of the war in the Middle East and thus energy prices,
have since crystallised. The post-meeting communication
and minutes also pointed to the July CPI result being above
expectations, as was June quarter GDP growth “at the margin”.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction

costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data
centre boom. No weight is put on the possibility that this new
investment and technology might add to productivity or the
capital stock, and so supply capacity, even with a lag. Indeed,
the post-meeting minutes highlighted the financial stability
risks if valuations in this area should disappoint “even if the
technology were ultimately successful”, as if the latter was not
yet clear. This language points to a generally downbeat view of

Australian supply capacity that also emerges in its discussion
of productivity trends. Although its position piece in the August
2025 Statement on Monetary Policy  indicated that lower trend
growth in productivity had no implications for inflation or
monetary policy, more recent statements imply that it does.
More broadly, the minutes for the September meeting revealed
a tendency to contemplate more hawkish interpretations
of data and outcomes. For example, the minutes dismissed

evidence of the labour market easing a bit more than
was forecast by pointing to the changes to the survey
arrangements. At the same time, the minutes contemplated
that the rate of unemployment consistent with inflation
being at target might have risen, even though wages growth
has marginally undershot the RBA’s forecasts and the cost
pressures cited by its business liaison contacts are mostly
non-labour costs. The minutes also included an extended

discussion of the possibility that the global neutral interest
rate might have increased recently. If true, this would imply
that financial conditions were less restrictive. This conclusion
would be hard to square with yield curves that have flattened
since the beginning of the year and other central bank
estimates of the term premium having increased, both of which
suggest tighter financial conditions.
That said, the RBA will find it hard to look past inflation

outcomes in the second half of year that is likely to remain
sticky. We anticipate trimmed mean inflation to record
quarterly outcomes of around 0.9%qtr in Q3 and Q4, remaining
around the current 3½%yr pulse. The Monetary Policy Board
will want to see clear evidence of inflation coming down before
withdrawing policy restrictiveness, so we continue to pencil in
August as the likely date for start of the rate-cutting phase.

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
After posting a soft but better than expected first half, the
Australian economy is facing into more challenges near term
as another round of disruptions from global energy markets
combine with a further rise in interest rates and a deepening

housing downturn. Against this, business investment is in
the midst of a sustained ramp-up led by a large and growing
pipeline of data centre and energy investment projects.
Elsewhere, public demand is growing at a slower pace and
new dwelling investment is likely to moderate but both are
expected to still see positive growth. Overall, the demand
mix should see capacity pressures continue to ease but with
significant risks on either side.
Shifting political winds

Some of these risks are now in the wider policy space. The
political landscape has changed materially since the 2025
Federal Election with opinion polls showing a sustained swing
against the Labor government and a marked rise in support
for One Nation that raises the prospect of a three-horse race
in the next election, the outcome of which could be very hard
to predict (we may get a preview of this with next month’s
Victorian state election). While the next Federal election is

still a long way off – not due until well into 2028 – the shift
does appear to be having some bearing on policy. For example,
the government’s choice not to reinstate the temporary
halving in fuel excise tax that was deployed when fuel prices
first spiked back in March suggests it is less inclined towards
providing broad based cost-of-living support that might make
it harder to bring inflation back down.
Migration policy is shaping more clearly as a battleground.

A suite of changes announced by the government in mid-
September and which came into effect in October, are already
expected to slow working holidaymaker and foreign student
arrivals, moderating total net overseas migration from
around 300k current to 225k by 2028, trimming population
growth from around 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr. For its part, the main
Liberal-National opposition party has proposed to reduce net
migration to 100k in the first two years of government, before

increasing to 130k and 160k in the two years following –
which would likely see total population growth slow to around
0.7-0.8%yr initially. One Nation has proposed a period of ‘net
negative’ migration, lowering net migration by 250k a year
over the first three years of government before considering a
‘ceiling’ of 130k per year thereafter. This would see population
growth slow to around 0.2-0.3%yr initially.
While none of these policies are on the table for this year

or next, the direction of travel is clear with a risk that the
current government moves further towards curbing migrant
More challenges near term ...
Matthew Hassan, Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting
inflows. Slower population growth basically maps one-to-
one to GDP growth although the specific flow-on impacts to
areas like housing and labour markets are more complex and
will depend on the population mix and how the demand and
supply effects balance out. For the record, we have trimmed

our own population growth forecasts in the 2028 out-year,
from 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr with a related tweak to our GDP
growth forecast for that year.
Consumer sentiment-spending gap
Near term challenges continue to centre on the consumer.
Here, the latest updates show sentiment again plumbing
historical lows but more mixed messages around spending
which is still holding up better than might be expected based
on a simple read on the consumer mood.
Certainly that mood remains deeply negative. The Westpac–

Melbourne Institute Consumer Sentiment Index dropped
4.7% to 80.4 in October, putting the month amongst the forty
worst reads since the began in the early 1970s, following
two others reads in this group back in April and June and
another eight recorded in 2022–23. Altogether this marks the
worst period of recurring extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
This has been difficult to square with the soft rather than

outright weak picture on consumer spending through most
of this year. Even when real spending was recording more
material per capita declines back in 2023-24, these were
somewhat milder than what might have been expected given
the weakness in sentiment at the time.
We explored this more fully in our August Red Book . Some
of the disconnect may be a by-product of high inflation which
‘hits different’ sentiment-wise. Cost of living pressures,

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... centred on uneven consumer, housing downturn ...
which are the central issue for consumers at the moment,
represent a broad-based but shallower squeeze rather than
the deeper shocks that typically come when big economic
downturns hit. Labour markets appear to be an important

point of distinction – despite the weakness in sentiment,
consumers have been relatively unperturbed about the jobs
outlook in recent years. Adding consumers’ unemployment
expectations to sentiment measures significantly improves its
‘fit’ as a predictor of per capita spending.
There other aspect that helps to reconcile the sentiment-
spending gap is the detailed picture across segments. There
may be one economy but there is not a single consumer.

Instead both sentiment and spending outcomes reflect the
average across a range of often quite varied conditions in
different consumer segments.
This shows through in the dispersion of sentiment across
sub-groups. The chart right shows the current distribution of
index reads across the 100-odd sub-groups we regularly track
with some selected groups called out. If the range seems wide
its because it is – measures of spread over the last two years
have been 50-60% wider than the average over the previous

three decades. Exactly why is not completely clear but the
observations point to a more fractured consumer with multiple
fault lines – including across states, metro/regional areas, voter
groups, labour force status, income levels and housing tenures.
Spending-wise, one of the clearest emerging fault lines is across
age groups. Here, there look to be three main ‘blocs’ – 18-34 year
olds (mainly millennials’); 35-54 year olds (mainly ‘gen X’); and

those aged over 55 (dominated by the ‘baby boomers’).
Demographically, the 55+ age group is both the largest and
the fastest-growing – our estimates suggest it now makes up
about 45% of all households, up 8.5pts compared to 2020. It
is also seeing significant financial shifts with the cohort coming
through carrying materially larger superannuation holdings
(reflecting a variety of factors including a longer period of
compulsory contributions that have earned compounding

returns) and more housing wealth. Again, our rough estimates
suggest average household wealth in this group is around
$2.5m up over $800k since 2020 (reflecting both the
aforementioned demographic shifts and valuation changes).
At the same time, the group has low debt levels – it is largely
untouched by interest rate increases and probably benefits
slightly insofar as this lifts interest income both directly and
indirectly via super holdings.
For 18-34 year olds, incomes are more important than balance

sheets, meaning inflation poses more of a constraint. For 35-
54 year olds, income is less of a constraint but high debt levels
poses a big challenge as rate rises increase servicing costs.
All of which is to say that it should come as little surprise that
spending by the 55+ age group has been running stronger
than the other age groups. Breakdowns from our Westpac
Consumer Panel show average non fuel spend has been rising
at a 5% annual pace over the last six months amongst those

These demographic contrasts also account for the observed 
strength in key bellwether spending categories like new 
vehicle purchases. The total number of new car purchases 
by consumers is up close to 4%yr, led by strong growth in

households rather than a new addition). 
Pulling these clues together, at least some of the resilience in 
spending looks to be a form of ‘wealth effect’ where balance 
sheet considerations are more important than the real purchasing 
power of incomes. While the impact of employment, wages, 
inflation and interest rate changes will still likely be the dominant 
driver of shifts in spending growth, the nature of this wealth 
effect and how it evolves from here could be a key swing factor. 
Housing downturn deepens
And where that swing factor may start to turn is in response 
to a deepening downturn in Australia’s housing market. The 
combined impact of interest rate rises and major changes to 
housing-related tax policy announced in the Federal budget is 
continuing to weigh heavily. We have been flagging for some 
time that uncertainty could see a more significant ‘air pocket’ 
emerge with very thin trading conditions and a sharper move 
lower in prices. That risk is crystallising with turnover already 
down 20% and prices off 8%. 
With the September interest rate rise and an expected hike 
in November still to come, more weakness is likely near term. 
We revised our house price forecasts lower with a decline of 
around 8% now expected for calendar 2026, with a peak-to-
trough decline of just over 10%. 
... wealth effects an uncertain swing factor 
This constitutes a more material housing downturn. The 
peak to trough fall is set to be biggest since the beginning of 
the national price measures in 1980, although prices in real, 
inflation-adjusted terms recorded larger falls during the early-
1990s and early-1980s recessions with capital city measures 
also suggesting there were larger real price falls during the 
recessions in the mid-1970s and early 1960s. 
It also points to a bigger housing-related drag on growth. 
As discussed previously, the main channels are through 
new dwelling investment, turnover and wealth effects. New 
dwelling investment is still carrying solid momentum (the 
number of dwelling under construction up 10% over the year 
to June) but is expected to see a slightly more pronounced 
slowing. Turnover is also expected to be slightly weaker. 
However, it is the wealth effects from price declines that is the 
area to watch. Our rough estimates suggest this will account 
for most of the –0.8ppts on annual GDP growth over the 
year to June 2027. The emphasis here though is on ‘rough’. 
Wealth effects have seeming disappeared for long periods 
and their precise timing is very unclear, e.g. how should the 
drag from the current downturn be viewed against what 
were presumably strong positives coming off the much larger 
preceding gains? Some of this could be due to ‘assymmetric’ 
behaviour, with effects playing out very differently when there 
wealth is rising or falling.
On balance, we expect the impact to see consumer spending 
‘muddle through’ rather than fall back to the weaker growth 
seen during the more intense cost of living and interest 
rate shocks in 2023-24. This partly reflects the indirect 
support coming from growth more generally – the AI-related 
investment boom, which we have covered at length in previous 
reports, a key factor helping to hold GDP growth up around 
1.5%yr and prevent a more material weakening in labour 
market conditions. For the consumer though this will continue 
to feel like a struggle with growth flat in per capita terms 
and a shakier picture around housing that will play out very 
differently for some segments.

MIDDLE EAST
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should
ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could

push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating
global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite crude exports through the Strait of Hormuz

recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels in late
September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back
to normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings
have risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving
visible transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo
transfers in the Gulf of Oman have increased.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly.

Ship-to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts cause
delays and some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships
are also available, with many redeployed to other markets.
Higher insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-
China tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher
than a year ago. With prompt barrels scarce, the gap between
physical and futures prices widened again, with Dated Brent
around US$136/bbl in early October against US$101/bbl for

front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes
on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the

Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened to
an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October, from
US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the government’s excise
tax cut returning to normal, this has lifted national diesel and
petrol prices to around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a

return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average
around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around

US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of this
reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi attacks
on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below its

WESTPAC ECONOMICS

... as supply still tight ...
With product supply recovering slowly and depleted inventories
being rebuilt, Singapore gasoil crack is likely to stay above its
long-run average even as Brent eases, narrowing from US$71/
bbl in Q4 to US$27/bbl by Q4 2027. With Singapore Gas

10ppm prices, shipping and insurance costs to remain elevated
and easing only gradually in 2027, we forecast Australian diesel
to retail at an average of 274¢/L in Q4, easing to 200¢/L in Q4
2027, and petrol at 230¢/L and 187¢/L respectively.
LNG prices supported through 2027
Japanese LNG prices surged 15.2% in September, averaging
US$25.4/mmbtu as supply disruptions in Qatar and the Strait
of Hormuz tightened markets and intensified competition.

Our Q4 forecast for Japanese LNG is now US$24.5/mmbtu
and we expect the market to remain structurally tight, with
supply recovery likely to be gradual and prices not returning
to pre-conflict levels until 2028. Still, Australia’s domestic
gas market should remain insulated due to the current gas
reservation arrangements.
Risks around our oil price outlook are two-sided
Talks are still ongoing in the background, with the US and
Iran exchanging proposals to reopen the Strait, but the gap

between them remains wide. The conflict has also expanded
to the Red Sea and, with inventories sharply drawn down,
there is a smaller buffer to absorb a further shock.
Using the Oxford Economics model, we assess an escalation
scenario, where energy markets face a deeper and more
persistent disruption as the conflict in the Middle East
intensifies. In this case, Brent futures rise to around US$150/
bbl in H1 2027, only dipping to below US$100/bbl in Q2 2028.

We also consider an early resolution scenario, where shipping
and Gulf supply normalise faster, returning Brent to near pre-
conflict levels by end-2027.
Escalation scenario
In the escalation scenario we assume:
Talks stall and the conflict escalates:  the gap between the
US and Iran proves too wide and the US resumes strikes after
the midterms. Iran responds with attacks on shipping and Gulf
energy infrastructure, damaging export, storage and refining

facilities. A lasting agreement is not reached until 2028.
Export routes are disrupted: more frequent attacks curtail
dark crossings of the Strait and ship-to-ship transfers in the
Gulf of Oman. Houthi attacks also cut off Saudi alternative
routes, forcing Gulf producers to cut output and Middle
East crude exports sharply lower. New routes and shipping
arrangements eventually support a recovery in Gulf flows, but
trade patterns take time to adjust.

Product supply fails to recover:  The Russia–Ukraine energy
ceasefire does not hold and strikes on Russian refineries
pre-conflict level until H2 2027. Meanwhile, we look for UAE
production to continue to improve over 2027 and lift above
pre-conflict levels from 2028, as it is no longer constrained by
OPEC quotas. We do not expect a meaningful return of Iranian
oil until an agreement is reached, and damage sustained
during the conflict means production will take time to recover.

We expect global oil demand to be 103.7mb/d in 2026, 0.8mb/d
(0.7%) higher than in 2025, and to rise a further 1.9mb/d (1.8%)
to 105.6mb/d in 2027. Chinese crude imports will continue to
recover over the coming months as refiners restock ahead of
Lunar New Year, but we expect only a gradual return to pre-
conflict levels, as the electrification of transport limits the
recovery in Chinese demand. Globally, demand for diesel and jet
fuel recovers as transport and aviation activity picks up, although

some demand destruction and efficiency gains are expected
to be permanent. The conflict has also highlighted strategic
vulnerabilities, particularly across Asia, so we expect a push to
rebuild reserves adding around 0.5% to demand over 2027.
Fuel prices to ease more slowly than crude
Refined product supply is expected to recover more slowly
than crude, with Gulf refinery runs not returning to pre-war
levels until at least late 2027. Outside the Gulf, there has

been some constructive news. The US has lifted sanctions
on Russian diesel until April 2027, with Russia saying it will
supply more than 4Mt over the coming months, while China
has lifted its ban on refined product exports. However, we
are cautious about how much this will add to the market in
the short term. Much of the Russian supply is headed to the
US and will depend on Russia’s ability to restore refining and
export capacity, while doubts remain over the Russia-Ukraine

energy ceasefire announced by President Trump. China still
manages exports through quotas, which are likely to increase
only gradually as domestic stocks are rebuilt. Refinery runs
should lift ahead of Lunar New Year, but any increase in
exports will take time to reach regional markets.

Prices fall quickly, but restocking provides a floor:  Brent
Brent futures ease to around US$88/bbl in Q1 2027 as the
premium for prompt physical barrels unwinds. As the market
moves into balance, Brent eases to around US$65/bbl by Q4

Refined product prices ease faster on lower freight costs with

Economic impact
In the escalation scenario, higher fuel costs flow through to
transport, food and other energy-intensive goods such as
plastics and fertilisers. Australian headline CPI remains above
4.5% for much of 2027, with underlying inflation remaining
sticky at above 3% until late 2028. The RBA faces a difficult

trade off between much higher inflation and a significant
hit to activity, as households cut discretionary spending and
businesses defer investment. Higher LNG export earnings
provide only a moderate offset. In the early resolution
scenario, headline inflation is around 0.1ppt below the
baseline in 2027.
New Zealand is more exposed in both scenarios, with food
and energy a larger share of its CPI and no offsetting energy
exports. Headline inflation is around 1ppt higher in the

escalation scenario in Q1 2027.
continue, limiting how much diesel Russia can export,
even with the US sanctions waiver extended. China also

Oil prices lift to around US$150/bbl in H1 2027: With little
buffer to absorb the supply loss, competition for physical
delivery intensifies, with physical prices well above futures
as prompt barrels remain scarce. Higher prices support
supply growth outside OPEC and drive demand destruction,

2027, $9.1 above our baseline.
Early resolution scenario

In the early resolution scenario we assume:
A durable agreement: The US and Iran agree terms to reopen
the Strait by the end of year, and, unlike the June interim deal,
the agreement holds, with progress towards a permanent
settlement in early 2027. Attacks on shipping cease and the
US blockade is lifted, allowing Iranian oil back into the market,
initially from storage. Vessels are quickly repositioned to the
Gulf, with freight and war-risk insurance easing from early

2027 rather than mid-year.
A faster recovery in supply:  With export routes secure, Gulf
production and damaged refinery and export capacity recover
sooner than in the baseline. Outside the Gulf, the US extends
its waiver on Russian diesel beyond April 2027 and China
raises export quotas as domestic stocks are rebuilt.
